Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A boy named Ayrat lives on planet AMI-1511. Each inhabitant of this planet has a talent. Specifically, Ayrat loves running, moreover, just running is not enough for him. He is dreaming of making running a real art.

First, he wants to construct the running track with coating t. On planet AMI-1511 the coating of the track is the sequence of colored blocks, where each block is denoted as the small English letter. Therefore, every coating can be treated as a string.

Unfortunately, blocks aren't freely sold to non-business customers, but Ayrat found an infinite number of coatings s. Also, he has scissors and glue. Ayrat is going to buy some coatings s, then cut out from each of them exactly one continuous piece (substring) and glue it to the end of his track coating. Moreover, he may choose to flip this block before glueing it. Ayrat want's to know the minimum number of coating s he needs to buy in order to get the coating t for his running track. Of course, he also want's to know some way to achieve the answer.


-----Input-----

First line of the input contains the string s — the coating that is present in the shop. Second line contains the string t — the coating Ayrat wants to obtain. Both strings are non-empty, consist of only small English letters and their length doesn't exceed 2100.


-----Output-----

The first line should contain the minimum needed number of coatings n or -1 if it's impossible to create the desired coating.

If the answer is not -1, then the following n lines should contain two integers x_{i} and y_{i} — numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece. If x_{i} ≤ y_{i} then this piece is used in the regular order, and if x_{i} > y_{i} piece is used in the reversed order. Print the pieces in the order they should be glued to get the string t.


-----Examples-----
Input
abc
cbaabc

Output
2
3 1
1 3

Input
aaabrytaaa
ayrat

Output
3
1 1
6 5
8 7

Input
ami
no

Output
-1



-----Note-----

In the first sample string "cbaabc" = "cba" + "abc".

In the second sample: "ayrat" = "a" + "yr" + "at".

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a string `s` that we can buy and use
2. From each purchased coating, we can cut out exactly one continuous substring and either use it as-is or in reverse
3. We want to build target string `t` by concatenating these pieces
4. We need to find the minimum number of coatings needed, and if possible, provide the segments and their direction (x,y where x≤y means original order, x>y means reversed)

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains a character that doesn't exist in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We need to find the minimum number of pieces from `s` that can form `t`
3. For each position in `t`, we need to find a substring of `s` that matches either the original or reversed version
4. This is essentially a string matching problem where we need to decompose `t` into segments that are substrings of `s` or their reverse
5. We can use dynamic programming or greedy approach

## Algorithm Approach

1. First, check if all characters in `t` exist in `s`. If not, return -1
2. For each position in `t`, try to match the remaining suffix with substrings of `s` or reversed `s`
3. Use DP to find the minimum number of pieces needed

However, given the constraints (length up to 2100), we need an efficient solution.

Actually, let me think of this differently:

Since we can only use substrings from `s`, and we can reverse them, we need to check if `t` can be segmented such that each segment is either a substring of `s` or a reversed substring of `s`.

We can use dynamic programming where:
- `dp[i]` = minimum number of pieces needed to form first `i` characters of `t`
- For each position, try all possible lengths of the next piece

## Step-by-Step Solution

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_chars = set(s)
    t_chars = set(t)
    if not t_chars.issubset(s_chars):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = len(t)
    
    # Precompute all substrings and their reverses
    # For efficiency, we'll use DP with backtracking
    dp = [float('inf')] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 0
    
    # parent[i] will store the previous position and the substring used
    parent = [None] * (n + 1)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible lengths of the last piece
        for j in range(1, i + 1):
            # Check if substring t[i-j:i] can be formed from s or reversed s
            substr = t[i-j:i]
            found = False
            
            # Check if substr exists in s (original)
            if substr in s:
                if dp[i-j] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[i-j] + 1
                    parent[i] = (i-j, j, 'original')
                    found = True
            
            # Check if reverse of substr exists in s
            rev_substr = substr[::-1]
            if rev_substr in s:
                if dp[i-j] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[i-j] + 1
                    parent[i] = (i-j, j, 'reversed')
                    found = True
    
    if dp[n] == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Reconstruct the solution
    result = []
    pos = n
    while pos > 0:
        prev, length, direction = parent[pos]
        if direction == 'original':
            # Add segment from pos-length to pos-1 (in original order)
            result.append((prev + 1, prev + length))  # x,y where x <= y
        else:  # reversed
            # Add segment from pos-length to pos-1 (in reversed order)
            # Need to flip the indices
            result.append((prev + 1, prev + length))
        
        pos = prev
    
    result.reverse()
    
    print(dp[n])
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with my indexing. Let me reconsider the problem.

Looking at the examples:
- Example 1: s="abc", t="cbaabc"
- We need "cba" + "abc"
- "cba" is reverse of "abc" (reverse of substring from 1 to 3)
- "abc" is substring from 1 to 3

Let me fix the solution:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_chars = set(s)
    t_chars = set(t)
    if not t_chars.issubset(s_chars):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = len(t)
    
    # dp[i] = minimum number of pieces to form first i characters of t
    dp = [float('inf')] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 0
    
    # parent[i] = (start_pos, end_pos, direction) where direction is 0 for original, 1 for reversed
    parent = [None] * (n + 1)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible segment lengths ending at position i
        for length in range(1, i + 1):
            # Segment from i-length to i-1
            segment = t[i-length:i]
            
            # Check if segment exists as substring in s (original)
            if segment in s:
                if dp[i-length] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[i-length] + 1
                    parent[i] = (i-length, length, 0)  # 0 for original
            
            # Check if reverse segment exists in s
            rev_segment = segment[::-1]
            if rev_segment in s:
                if dp[i-length] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[i-length] + 1
                    parent[i] = (i-length, length, 1)  # 1 for reversed
    
    if dp[n] == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Reconstruct the solution
    result = []
    pos = n
    while pos > 0:
        prev, length, direction = parent[pos]
        if direction == 0:  # original
            result.append((prev + 1, prev + length))
        else:  # reversed
            result.append((prev + 1, prev + length))
        
        pos = prev
    
    result.reverse()
    
    print(dp[n])
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

- s = "abc", t = "cbaabc"
- We want to get "cba" + "abc"

For "cba": 
- Reverse of "abc" is "cba", which is in s
- So we use position 1,3 in reverse order, meaning x=1, y=3 with x>y

For "abc":
- "abc" is in s, so we use position 1,3 in original order

But in my code, for a segment of length 3 from position 4 to 6 (i=6, length=3), we get t[3:6]="abc", which is correct.

Actually, I think the indexing is off. Let me fix it:

```python
s = input().strip()
t = input().strip()

# Check if all characters in t exist in s
s_chars = set(s)
t_chars = set(t)
if not t_chars.issubset(s_chars):
    print(-1)
else:
    n = len(t)
    
    # dp[i] = minimum number of pieces to form first i characters of t
    dp = [float('inf')] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 0
    
    # parent[i] = (start_index, length, direction) where direction 0=original, 1=reversed
    parent = [None] * (n + 1)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible segment lengths ending at position i
        for length in range(1, i + 1):
            # Segment from i-length to i-1 (0-indexed)
            segment = t[i-length:i]
            
            # Check if segment exists in s
            if segment in s:
                if dp[i-length] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[i-length] + 1
                    parent[i] = (i-length, length, 0)  # original
            
            # Check if reverse segment exists in s
            rev_segment = segment[::-1]
            if rev_segment in s:
                if dp[i-length] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[i-length] + 1
                    parent[i] = (i-length, length, 1)  # reversed
    
    if dp[n] == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
    else:
        # Reconstruct solution
        result = []
        pos = n
        while pos > 0:
            start, length, direction = parent[pos]
            # The segment is from start+1 to start+length in the original string
            # For original: x=start+1, y=start+length
            # For reversed: x=start+length, y=start+1 (since we reverse it)
            if direction == 0:
                result.append((start + 1, start + length))
            else:  # direction == 1 (reversed)
                result.append((start + length, start + 1))  # x > y
            
            pos = start
        
        result.reverse()
        
        print(dp[n])
        for x, y in result:
            print(x, y)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a coating string `s`
2. We can cut out any substring from `s` and either use it in original order or reversed order
3. We want to build target string `t` by concatenating these substrings
4. We need to find the minimum number of such substrings needed

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains any character that doesn't exist in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We need to find the minimum number of substrings from `s` that can form `t`
3. Each substring can be used in original order (x ≤ y) or reversed order (x > y)
4. We want to maximize the length of each substring to minimize the count

## Algorithm Strategy

1. First check if all characters in `t` exist in `s`. If not, return -1.
2. Use a greedy approach: try to match as long as possible from the start of `t`.
3. For each position, try to find the longest substring from `s` that can match the remaining part of `t` (either directly or in reverse).
4. Use dynamic programming or greedy matching from left to right.

## Detailed Approach

1. Check if all characters in `t` are present in `s`. If not, return -1.
2. Use a greedy algorithm: iterate through `t` and for each position, find the longest possible substring from `s` that can match the current segment of `t`.
3. For each segment, we can either use it normally or reversed.
4. We need to check if the substring from `s` can match the current part of `t` or its reverse.

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    t_set = set(t)
    if not t_set.issubset(s_set):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    
    # For each position in t, we need to find a substring from s that matches
    # either directly or reversed
    
    i = 0
    while i < n:
        # Try to find the longest substring from s that can match t[i:]
        found = False
        j = i
        
        # Try all possible substrings from s
        # We'll try to match as much as possible from position i
        while j < n:
            # Look for substring in s that matches t[i:j+1] or reverse of it
            # We need to check if there's a substring in s that can match t[i:j+1]
            
            # Try to find the substring that matches t[i:j+1] in forward or backward
            # We'll check if there's a substring in s that can match this
            
            # Check if t[i:j+1] exists in s or in reverse
            substr = t[i:j+1]
            substr_rev = substr[::-1]
            
            # Check if substr exists in s or substr_rev exists in s
            if substr in s or substr_rev in s:
                # If we found a match, use it
                result.append((i+1, j+1))  # 1-indexed
                i = j + 1
                found = True
                break
            j += 1
        
        if not found:
            # Try to find a match for the current position
            # Look for any single character match
            current_char = t[i]
            if current_char in s:
                # Find the first occurrence of current_char in s
                first_pos = s.find(current_char)
                if first_pos != -1:
                    result.append((first_pos + 1, first_pos + 1))
                    i += 1
                else:
                    print(-1)
                    return
            else:
                print(-1)
                return
    
    # Actually, the above approach might not be optimal
    # Let me reconsider with a different greedy approach
    
    # Better approach: Try to match as much as possible from each position
    i = 0
    result = []
    
    while i < n:
        # Try to find the longest substring of t[i:] that can be formed from s
        # by either direct or reverse match
        
        max_match = 0
        best_start = i
        best_end = i
        
        # Try each possible end position
        for end in range(i, n):
            substr = t[i:end+1]
            substr_rev = substr[::-1]
            
            # Check if substr or substr_rev exists in s
            if substr in s or substr_rev in s:
                max_match = end - i + 1
                best_start = i
                best_end = end
        
        if max_match == 0:
            # No match found, try single character
            current_char = t[i]
            if current_char not in s:
                print(-1)
                return
            
            # Find first occurrence of current_char in s
            first_pos = s.find(current_char)
            result.append((first_pos + 1, first_pos + 1))
            i += 1
        else:
            # Found a match, use it
            substr = t[best_start:best_end+1]
            substr_rev = substr[::-1]
            
            # Check which one is in s
            if substr in s:
                result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
            else:
                result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
            i = best_end + 1
    
    print(len(result))
    for pos in result:
        print(pos[0], pos[1])

solve()
```

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me reconsider the problem.

Actually, the key insight is that we have infinite copies of string `s`, and we can extract any substring from any copy. We need to build `t` as a concatenation of substrings from `s` (either original or reversed).

The greedy approach should be:
1. For each position in `t`, find the longest substring of `s` that can match the current segment of `t` (either directly or reversed)
2. Use that substring
3. Move to the next position

But I need to be more careful. Let me implement a cleaner version:

```python
s = input().strip()
t = input().strip()

# Check if all characters in t exist in s
if not set(t).issubset(set(s)):
    print(-1)
else:
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < n:
        # Try to match as much as possible from position i
        found = False
        
        # Try each possible end position for current match
        for j in range(i, n):
            substr = t[i:j+1]
            substr_rev = substr[::-1]
            
            # Check if we can match this substring (directly or reversed) in s
            if substr in s or substr_rev in s:
                # We found a match, use it
                if substr in s:
                    # Use original order
                    result.append((i+1, j+1))
                else:
                    # Use reversed order
                    result.append((i+1, j+1))
                
                i = j + 1
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            # Should not happen given our subset check
            print(-1)
            exit()
    
    print(len(result))
    for r in result:
        print(r[0], r[1])
```

3. The greedy approach: build the string from left to right, at each position, find the shortest possible piece that can extend our current string

## Algorithm Steps

Wait, I think there's a simpler greedy approach:

- Start with empty string
- For each position in `t`, extend by finding a substring of `s` that matches the remaining part of `t`
- Try to extend as much as possible in each step (greedy)

Let me implement a solution:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_chars = set(s)
    t_chars = set(t)
    if not t_chars.issubset(s_chars):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Build result using greedy approach
    result = []
    current_pos = 0
    
    while current_pos < len(t):
        # Try to extend from current_pos as much as possible
        best_len = 0
        best_start = -1
        best_end = -1
        
        # Try all possible substrings of s
        # We need to find a substring of s that matches t[current_pos:]
        # This substring can be used in original or reversed order
        
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start, len(s)):
                sub = s[start:end+1]
                rev = s[start:end+1][::-1]
                
                # Check if sub matches t[current_pos:]
                if len(sub) <= len(t) - current_pos and t[current_pos:current_pos + len(sub)] == sub:
                    if len(sub) > best_len:
                        best_len = len(sub)
                        best_start = start
                        best_end = end
                
                # Check if reversed sub matches t[current_pos:]
                if len(rev) <= len(t) - current_pos and t[current_pos:current_pos + len(rev)] == rev:
                    if len(rev) > best_len:
                        best_len = len(rev)
                        best_start = start
                        best_end = end
        
        # If no match found, impossible (should not happen given our check)
        if best_len == 0:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        # Add this piece to result
        result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))  # 1-indexed
        current_pos += best_len
    
    print(len(result))
    for start, end in result:
        print(start, end)
```

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_chars = set(s)
    t_chars = set(t)
    if not t_chars.issubset(s_chars):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    result = []
    current_pos = 0
    
    while current_pos < len(t):
        # Try to extend as much as possible
        max_possible = len(t) - current_pos
        found = False
        
        # Check all possible lengths from longest to shortest (greedy)
        for length in range(max_possible, 0, -1):
            # Check if any substring of length 'length' of s can match t[current_pos:current_pos+length]
            # or its reverse
            
            # Check if there's a substring in s that matches t[current_pos:current_pos+length]
            for start in range(len(s) - length + 1):
                substr = s[start:start + length]
                if substr == t[current_pos:current_pos + length]:
                    result.append((start + 1, start + length))
                    current_pos += length
                    found = True
                    break
                
                # Check if reverse matches
                rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                if rev_substr == t[current_pos:current_pos + length]:
                    result.append((start + 1, start + length))
                    current_pos += length
                    found = True
                    break
            
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    print(len(result))
    for start, end in result:
        print(start, end)
```

A boy named Ayrat lives on planet AMI-1511. Each inhabitant of this planet has a talent. Specifically, Ayrat loves running, moreover, just running is not enough for him. He is dreaming of making running a real art.

First, he wants to construct the running track with coating t. On planet AMI-1511 the coating of the track is the sequence of colored blocks, where each block is denoted as the small English letter. Therefore, every coating can be treated as a string.

Unfortunately, blocks aren't freely sold to non-business customers, but Ayrat found an infinite number of coatings s. Also, he has scissors and glue. Ayrat is going to buy some coatings s, then cut out from each of them exactly one continuous piece (substring) and glue it to the end of his track coating. Moreover, he may choose to flip this block before glueing it. Ayrat want's to know the minimum number of coating s he needs to buy in order to get the coating t for his running track. Of course, he also want's to know some way to achieve the answer.


-----Input-----

First line of the input contains the string s — the coating that is present in the shop. Second line contains the string t — the coating Ayrat wants to obtain. Both strings are non-empty, consist of only small English letters and their length doesn't exceed 2100.


-----Output-----

The first line should contain the minimum needed number of coatings n or -1 if it's impossible to create the desired coating.

If the answer is not -1, then the following n lines should contain two integers x_{i} and y_{i} — numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece. If x_{i} ≤ y_{i} then this piece is used in the regular order, and if x_{i} > y_{i} piece is used in the reversed order. Print the pieces in the order they should be glued to get the string t.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a base string `s` and want to create target string `t`
2. From each base string, we can cut out a contiguous substring and optionally flip it (reverse it)
3. We need to find the minimum number of such operations to create `t`
4. If impossible, return -1

## Key Insights

1. For each position in `t`, we need to find a substring from `s` that can match it either in original or reversed form
2. We need to find the minimum number of substrings to concatenate to form `t`
3. This is essentially a string matching problem with the ability to reverse substrings

## Algorithm Approach

1. First check if it's possible: for every character in `t`, it must appear in `s`. If any character in `t` is not in `s`, return -1.

2. Use dynamic programming or greedy approach to build `t` from `s` substrings:
   - For each position in `t`, try to match the longest possible substring from `s` that can match the remaining suffix (either in original or reversed form)
   - Use the fact that we can reverse substrings

3. Actually, this can be simplified: we need to check if `t` can be constructed from substrings of `s` (original or reversed).

## Detailed Solution Steps

1. Check if every character in `t` exists in `s`. If not, return -1.

2. Use a greedy approach: for each position in `t`, find the longest substring of `s` that can match the current suffix of `t` (either directly or in reverse).

3. Build the solution by iterating through `t` and finding matching substrings.

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if every character in t exists in s
    s_set = set(s)
    for char in t:
        if char not in s_set:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    
    # For each position in t, we need to find a substring from s
    # that matches either directly or in reverse
    
    i = 0
    while i < n:
        # Try to find the longest possible match starting at position i
        found = False
        
        # First try to match directly
        j = i
        while j < n:
            # Try to find a substring of s that matches t[i:j+1] or reverse of it
            substring = t[i:j+1]
            
            # Check if substring exists in s (or its reverse)
            if substring in s or substring[::-1] in s:
                # We found a match, but we need to make sure it's valid
                # Actually, we need to check if there's a substring in s that has the same content
                # as either substring or its reverse
                
                # Find if substring or reverse exists in s
                target = substring
                if target not in s and target[::-1] not in s:
                    # Try shorter segments
                    pass
                else:
                    # We need to find the actual match in s
                    found = True
                    break
            j += 1
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
        
        # Actually, let me reconsider the approach
        # We need to find minimum number of substrings
        
        # Greedy approach: for each position, find shortest possible match
        # that can generate the rest
        best_match_len = 0
        best_match_tail = None
        
        # Try all possible lengths from 1 to remaining length
        for length in range(1, n - i + 1):
            target = t[i:i+length]
            target_rev = target[::-1]
            
            # Check if target or target_rev is a substring of s
            if target in s or target_rev in s:
                # Try to see if this length works
                # Check if we can complete the rest with what's left
                if length == n - i:  # This is the last piece
                    # We just need to add this piece
                    # Find the actual indices in s
                    if target in s:
                        # Find first occurrence of target in s
                        idx = s.find(target)
                        result.append((idx + 1, idx + length))  # 1-indexed
                    else:
                        # target_rev in s
                        idx = s.find(target_rev)
                        result.append((idx + 1, idx + length))  # 1-indexed
                        # Actually, we need to reverse the segment
                        result[-1] = (idx + 1, idx + length)
                        # But we're using reverse, so we need to adjust
                        result[-1] = (idx + 1, idx + length)
                else:
                    # We can use this piece and continue
                    if target in s:
                        idx = s.find(target)
                        result.append((idx + 1, idx + length))
                    elif target_rev in s:
                        idx = s.find(target_rev)
                        result.append((idx + 1, idx + length))
                        # This piece is reversed
                        result[-1] = (idx + 1, idx + length)
                        
                i += length
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

# Actually, my approach is getting messy. Let me think simpler.

def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if every character in t exists in s
    s_set = set(s)
    for char in t:
        if char not in s_set:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # Find all substrings of s and their reverses
    # We want to build t greedily
    
    result = []
    i = 0
    n = len(t)
    
    while i < n:
        # Find the longest substring that can match from position i
        # Try lengths from 1 to remaining length
        found = False
        best_length = 1
        
        for length in range(1, n - i + 1):
            target = t[i:i+length]
            target_rev = target[::-1]
            
            # Check if target or target_rev exists in s
            if target in s or target_rev in s:
                # This length works
                best_length = length
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
        
        # Now we know we need a piece of length best_length
        # Find the actual indices in s
        target = t[i:i+best_length]
        if target in s:
            # Find first occurrence of target in s
            start_idx = s.find(target)
            result.append((start_idx + 1, start_idx + best_length))
        else:
            # target is not in s, but target_rev is
            target_rev = target[::-1]
            start_idx = s.find(target_rev)
            # This piece is reversed, so we need to flip it
            result.append((start_idx + 1, start_idx + best_length))
        
        i += best_length
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A boy named Ayrat lives on planet AMI-1511. Each inhabitant of this planet has a talent. Specifically, Ayrat loves running, moreover, just running is not enough for him. He is dreaming of making running a real art.

First, he wants to construct the running track with coating t. On planet AMI-1511 the coating of the track is the sequence of colored blocks, where each block is denoted as the small English letter. Therefore, every coating can be treated as a string.

Unfortunately, blocks aren't freely sold to non-business customers, but Ayrat found an infinite number of coatings s. Also, he has scissors and glue. Ayrat is going to buy some coatings s, then cut out from each of them exactly one continuous piece (substring) and glue it to the end of his track coating. Moreover, he may choose to flip this block before glueing it. Ayrat want's to know the minimum number of coating s he needs to buy in order to get the coating t for his running track. Of course, he also want's to know some way to achieve the answer.


-----Input-----

First line of the input contains the string s — the coating that is present in the shop. Second line contains the string t — the coating Ayrat wants to obtain. Both strings are non-empty, consist of only small English letters and their length doesn't exceed 2100.


-----Output-----

The first line should contain the minimum needed number of coatings n or -1 if it's impossible to create the desired coating.

If the answer is not -1, then the following n lines should contain two integers x_{i} and y_{i} — numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece. If x_{i} ≤ y_{i} then this piece is used in the regular order, and if x_{i} > y_{i} piece is used in the reversed order. Print the pieces in the order they should be glued to get the string t.


-----Examples-----
Input
abc
cbaabc

Output
2
3 1
1 3

Input
aaabrytaaa
ayrat

Output
3
1 1
6 5
8 7

Input
ami
no

Output
-1

In the first sample string "cbaabc" = "cba" + "abc".

In the second sample: "ayrat" = "a" + "yr" + "at".

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a coating string `s`
2. We can cut out any substring from `s` and either use it in original order or reverse it
3. We want to build target string `t` by concatenating these substrings
4. We need to find the minimum number of such substrings and how to use them

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains a character that doesn't appear in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We need to find substrings of `s` that can match parts of `t`
3. For each part of `t`, we need to find a substring in `s` that matches it (either directly or in reverse)
4. We want to minimize the total number of pieces

## Algorithm Approach

1. First check if all characters in `t` exist in `s`. If not, return -1.
2. Use dynamic programming or greedy approach to build `t` from left to right.
3. For each position in `t`, try to match as long a substring as possible from `s` (either forward or backward).
4. For each match, record the start and end indices in `s` (with the condition that if start > end, it's reversed).

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Check if all characters in `t` exist in `s`
2. Use greedy matching: for each position in `t`, try to extend as far as possible
3. For each match, determine if it's in original order or reversed
4. Return the sequence of operations

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    t_set = set(t)
    
    if not t_set.issubset(s_set):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Build result
    result = []
    i = 0  # current position in t
    
    while i < len(t):
        # Try to match substring of t starting at position i
        # Try all possible lengths of the next substring
        found = False
        
        for length in range(1, len(t) - i + 1):
            substring = t[i:i+length]
            
            # Check if substring exists in s in original order
            if substring in s:
                # Find the first occurrence of substring in s
                pos = s.find(substring)
                result.append((pos + 1, pos + length))
                i += length
                found = True
                break
            
            # Check if reverse exists in s in original order
            reversed_substring = substring[::-1]
            if reversed_substring in s:
                pos = s.find(reversed_substring)
                result.append((pos + 1, pos + length))
                # Since we're reversing, we need to adjust the indices
                # We're using the substring in reverse order
                # So (pos+1, pos+length) means from pos+1 to pos+length in reversed form
                i += length
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            # If no match found, it should be impossible (shouldn't happen given our check)
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # Output result
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of string `s` and want to create target string `t`
2. From each copy of `s`, we can cut out a continuous substring and either use it as-is or reverse it
3. We need to find the minimum number of such pieces to form `t`
4. If impossible, return -1

## Key Insights

1. Since we have infinite copies of `s`, we can use any substring of `s` in any order
2. For each position in `t`, we need to find a substring from `s` that can match the required pattern
3. We can use a substring in original order (x ≤ y) or reversed order (x > y)
4. The problem reduces to: can we partition `t` into segments where each segment is either a substring of `s` or its reverse?

## Algorithm Strategy

1. Check if it's possible: if `t` contains characters not in `s`, return -1
2. Use dynamic programming or greedy approach to find the minimum segments
3. For each position in `t`, try to extend the current segment by matching with substrings of `s`
4. For each potential segment, check if it can be formed by a substring of `s` or its reverse

## Step-by-step Solution

1. First, check if all characters in `t` exist in `s`. If not, return -1.
2. Use a greedy approach: for each position in `t`, try to match as long as possible with a substring of `s` or its reverse.
3. For each possible segment ending at current position, check if it can be formed from `s`.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    t_set = set(t)
    if not t_set.issubset(s_set):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    
    # Use greedy approach: try to match as long as possible at each step
    pos = 0
    
    while pos < n:
        # Try to find the longest possible substring starting at pos
        found = False
        for end in range(pos + 1, n + 1):
            segment = t[pos:end]
            
            # Check if this segment can be formed by a substring of s or its reverse
            found_segment = False
            
            # Check if segment exists in s (original order)
            for i in range(len(s)):
                for j in range(i, len(s)):
                    substr = s[i:j+1]
                    if substr == segment:
                        result.append((i + 1, j + 1))  # 1-indexed
                        pos = end
                        found_segment = True
                        break
                if found_segment:
                    break
            
            # If found in original order, we're done
            if found_segment:
                found = True
                break
            
            # Check if segment exists in reverse (reversed order)
            reversed_segment = segment[::-1]
            for i in range(len(s)):
                for j in range(i, len(s)):
                    substr = s[i:j+1]
                    if substr == reversed_segment:
                        result.append((i + 1, j + 1))  # 1-indexed, but reversed means x > y
                        # Actually, we need to indicate reversed order
                        # In the output, if x > y, it means reversed
                        if i > j:
                            result[-1] = (j + 1, i + 1)  # swap to make x > y
                        pos = end
                        found_segment = True
                        break
                if found_segment:
                    break
            
            # If found in reversed order, we're done
            if found_segment:
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

Actually, let me think again. The key insight is that we need to find the minimum number of pieces. This is a covering problem where we want to minimize the number of segments.

But there's a simpler insight: since we have infinite copies of `s`, any substring of `s` or its reverse is available. So we just need to check if we can form `t` using substrings from `s` or their reverses.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    t_set = set(t)
    if not t_set.issubset(s_set):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    
    pos = 0
    
    while pos < n:
        # Try to match from current position onwards
        # We'll try to find the shortest segment that can complete the current match
        found = False
        
        # Try all possible lengths from 1 to remaining length
        for length in range(1, n - pos + 1):
            segment = t[pos:pos + length]
            
            # Check if segment appears in s (original order)
            for i in range(len(s) - length + 1):
                if s[i:i + length] == segment:
                    result.append((i + 1, i + length))
                    pos += length
                    found = True
                    break
            
            # If found original, we're done with this iteration
            if found:
                break
                
            # Check if segment appears in s in reverse order
            reversed_segment = segment[::-1]
            for i in range(len(s) - length + 1):
                if s[i:i + length] == reversed_segment:
                    result.append((i + 1, i + length))
                    # For reversed order, we need to output x > y
                    # But in the description, x > y means reversed
                    # So we need to ensure the output is correct
                    # Actually, if we used reversed, we should output x > y
                    # But our indices are still correct, we just need to know it's reversed
                    # The problem says: if x <= y - regular, if x > y - reversed
                    # So we need to flip the indices if it's reversed
                    
                    # For reversed segment, x > y
                    result[-1] = (i + length, i + 1)  # x > y
                    pos += length
                    found = True
                    break
            
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A boy named Ayrat lives on planet AMI-1511. Each inhabitant of this planet has a talent. Specifically, Ayrat loves running, moreover, just running is not enough for him. He is dreaming of making running a real art.

First, he wants to construct the running track with coating t. On planet AMI-1511 the coating of the track is the sequence of colored blocks, where each block is denoted as the small English letter. Therefore, every coating can be treated as a string.

Unfortunately, blocks aren't freely sold to non-business customers, but Ayrat found an infinite number of coatings s. Also, he has scissors and glue. Ayrat is going to buy some coatings s, then cut out from each of them exactly one continuous piece (substring) and glue it to the end of his track coating. Moreover, he may choose to flip this block before glueing it. Ayrat want's to know the minimum number of coating s he needs to buy in order to get the coating t for his running track. Of course, he also want's to know some way to achieve the answer.


-----Input-----

First line of the input contains the string s — the coating that is present in the shop. Second line contains the string t — the coating Ayrat wants to obtain. Both strings are non-empty, consist of only small English letters and their length doesn't exceed 2100.


-----Output-----

The first line should contain the minimum needed number of coatings n or -1 if it's impossible to create the desired coating.

If the answer is not -1, then the following n lines should contain two integers x_{i} and y_{i} — numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece. If x_{i} ≤ y_{i} then this piece is used in the regular order, and if x_{i} > y_{i} piece is used in the reversed order. Print the pieces in the order they should be glued to get the string t.


-----Examples-----
Input
abc
cbaabc

Output
2
3 1
1 3

Input
aaabrytaaa
ayrat

Output
3
1 1
6 5
8 7

Input
ami
no

Output
-1



-----Note-----

In the first sample string "cbaabc" = "cba" + "abc".

In the second sample: "ayrat" = "a" + "yr" + "at".

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a coating string `s`
2. We can cut out any continuous substring from `s` and either use it in original order or reversed order
3. We need to find the minimum number of such substrings to form target string `t`
4. We need to output the positions (x, y) where x ≤ y means original order, x > y means reversed order

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains a character that doesn't exist in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We need to find the minimum number of substrings to build `t`
3. This is essentially a segmentation problem where we want to minimize the number of segments
4. For each segment, we can either take it as-is or reverse it

## Strategy

1. First check if all characters in `t` exist in `s` - if not, return -1
2. Find the minimum number of segments by using dynamic programming or greedy approach
3. For each position in `t`, try to extend the current substring as much as possible
4. For each possible substring, check if it exists in `s` (in original or reversed form)

## Algorithm

The key insight is that we can use a greedy approach:
- We want to minimize the number of pieces
- For each segment, we should try to make it as long as possible
- We need to check if a substring of `t` exists in `s` or its reverse

However, a simpler approach:
1. Check if all characters in `t` are present in `s`
2. Use a greedy segmentation: start from beginning of `t`, try to extend as much as possible
3. For each position, find the longest valid substring (in s or reversed)

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem is more complex because:
- We can choose any substring from `s`
- We can reverse it
- We need to find minimum number of such operations

Actually, let's think differently:
- We need to build `t` by concatenating pieces from `s` (original or reversed)
- We want to minimize the count
- This is a shortest path problem in string matching

Better approach:
1. Check if all characters in `t` exist in `s` - if not, return -1
2. Use dynamic programming where dp[i] = minimum number of pieces to build t[0:i]
3. For each position, try all possible ending points and check if the substring t[j:i] can be formed from s (either directly or reversed)

But given the constraints (length up to 2100), we need an efficient solution.

## Final Algorithm

1. First check if all characters in `t` are in `s` → if not, return -1
2. Use greedy segmentation with the following idea:
   - Start from position 0 in t
   - For each position, try to find the longest substring that exists in s or reversed in s
   - Use memoization or DP to find minimum number of pieces

Actually, let me implement a simpler greedy solution that should work:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    for char in t:
        if char not in s_set:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # Try to build t by finding minimum number of substrings from s
    # We'll use a greedy approach: try to extend as much as possible
    
    result = []
    pos = 0
    
    while pos < len(t):
        # Try to find the longest possible substring starting at pos
        found = False
        
        # Try all possible end positions from pos onwards
        for end in range(pos + 1, len(t) + 1):
            substring = t[pos:end]
            
            # Check if this substring exists in s (original or reversed)
            if substring in s:
                # Found in original form
                # Position in s (1-indexed)
                # Find the first occurrence of substring in s
                idx = s.find(substring)
                result.append((idx + 1, idx + len(substring)))  # 1-indexed
                pos = end
                found = True
                break
            elif substring[::-1] in s:
                # Found in reversed form
                idx = s.find(substring[::-1])
                result.append((idx + 1, idx + len(substring)))  # 1-indexed, but reversed
                pos = end
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            # Should not happen if all chars are in s
            print(-1)
            return
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a coating string `s`
2. We can cut out any substring from `s` and either use it in normal order or reversed order
3. We want to build target string `t` by concatenating these substrings
4. We need to find the minimum number of substrings needed

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains any character not present in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We need to check if we can build `t` using substrings from `s`
3. For efficiency, we should use the longest possible substrings when possible
4. We can use dynamic programming or greedy approach with string matching

## Algorithm Steps

1. **Check feasibility**: If any character in `t` is not in `s`, return -1
2. **Greedy approach**: Build `t` from left to right by finding the longest possible substring from `s` that matches the current prefix of `t`
3. **Handle reversals**: For each segment, check if we can match the remaining string using either normal or reversed substring from `s`
4. **Track positions**: For each piece used, record the start and end indices in `s`

## Detailed Solution

The key insight is that we need to find the minimum number of substrings. Since we want minimum count, we should use longer substrings when possible.

For each position in `t`, we check if a substring from `s` (either normal or reversed) can match the current segment.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if t can be formed from characters in s
    s_set = set(s)
    for char in t:
        if char not in s_set:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # We'll use a greedy approach: build t from left to right
    # For each segment of t, find the shortest possible substring from s (normal or reversed)
    
    result = []
    i = 0  # current position in t
    
    while i < len(t):
        # Try to match as much as possible from current position
        # We'll try to find the longest prefix that can be matched
        
        found = False
        j = i
        
        # Try to extend from current position i
        while j < len(t):
            # Try to find a substring in s that matches t[i:j+1] or its reverse
            substr = t[i:j+1]
            
            # Check if substr exists in s (normal order)
            if substr in s:
                # Found match in normal order
                result.append((i, j))  # positions in t
                i = j + 1
                found = True
                break
            
            # Check if reverse of substr exists in s
            rev_substr = substr[::-1]
            if rev_substr in s:
                # Found match in reversed order
                result.append((j+1, i))  # reversed, so we need to adjust indices
                i = j + 1
                found = True
                break
            
            j += 1
        
        if not found:
            # This shouldn't happen if characters are valid, but just in case
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # But the above approach may not be optimal for indices in s
    
    # Let me reconsider: we need to find how to represent t as concatenation of substrings from s
    # and give the indices in s
    
    # Better approach: check if t can be built directly
    # We need to find matches in s
    
    # For each segment in t, we need to find a substring of s that matches that segment
    # or its reverse
    
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(t):
        # Try to find the longest possible match starting at position i
        j = i
        found_match = False
        
        # We'll try different lengths from 1 to remaining length
        while j < len(t) and not found_match:
            substr = t[i:j+1]
            
            # Check if substr appears in s in normal order
            if substr in s:
                result.append((i, j))
                i = j + 1
                found_match = True
                break
            
            # Check if reverse appears in s
            rev_substr = substr[::-1]
            if rev_substr in s:
                result.append((j+1, i))  # this means reverse order
                i = j + 1
                found_match = True
                break
            
            j += 1
        
        if not found_match:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # The above gives us the segments in t, but we need the indices in s
    # Actually, the problem asks for the ending blocks in the corresponding piece in s
    # But we don't need to find specific indices in s for the substring, just that the substring exists
    
    # Actually, rereading: "numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece"
    # This means for each piece we extract, give the start and end indices in s
    
    # But we need to reconstruct the actual substring extraction
    
    # Let me restructure:
    
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(t):
        # Try to match from i to end of current segment
        for j in range(i, len(t)):
            substr = t[i:j+1]
            
            # Check normal order
            if substr in s:
                # We found a match, but we need to know which indices in s
                # Find the first occurrence of substr in s
                pos = s.find(substr)
                result.append((pos + 1, pos + len(substr)))  # 1-indexed
                i = j + 1
                break
            elif substr[::-1] in s:
                # Reverse match
                rev_pos = s.find(substr[::-1])
                result.append((rev_pos + 1, rev_pos + len(substr[::-1])))  # 1-indexed but in reverse order
                i = j + 1
                break
        else:
            # No match found
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # But the reverse case: we need to output (x,y) where x > y for reverse
    # When we reverse, we should output (start, end) with start > end
    
    # Actually, in the reverse case, we use the substring in reverse, so the indices are the same
    # but we just need to indicate it's reversed
    
    # Correction: in the output, if x <= y, normal order; if x > y, reversed order
    # So for a normal substring from position p to q in s: (p+1, q+1) with p+1 <= q+1
    # For a reversed substring from position p to q in s: we output (q+1, p+1) with q+1 > p+1
    
    # Let me fix the result:
    
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    # For each substring found, find actual positions in s
    while i < len(t):
        found = False
        for j in range(i, len(t)):
            substr = t[i:j+1]
            
            # Check normal order
            if substr in s:
                pos = s.find(substr)
                result.append((pos + 1, pos + len(substr)))
                i = j + 1
                found = True
                break
            
            # Check reverse order
            rev_substr = substr[::-1]
            if rev_substr in s:
                pos = s.find(rev_substr)
                result.append((pos + len(rev_substr), pos + 1))  # reversed: end > start
                i = j + 1
                found = True
                break
                
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # Output
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

The problem says "numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece" and gives examples.

Looking at the first example:
- Input: s="abc", t="cbaabc"
- Output: 2 pieces: (3,1) and (1,3)

For "cba": it's reverse of "abc", so we take substring from index 1 to 3 (1-indexed) of "abc" and reverse it: positions 1,2,3 → reverse → becomes cba
For "abc": we take substring from 1 to 3 in normal order → becomes abc

But (3,1) means start=3, end=1, which implies reverse order, and (1,3) means normal order.

So yes, when x > y, it's reversed.

But in my code, I'm not using the correct substring extraction.

```python

                # Find first occurrence of substr in s

                result.append((start_pos + 1, start_pos + len(substr)))

                found = True
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sorting method called "Coprime sort" where we can only swap two elements if they have a common prime factor.
2. We need to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order using this method.
3. The key insight is that we can only swap elements that share a common prime factor.

## Key Insight

The crucial observation is about connected components in a graph:
- Each number can be connected to other numbers that share at least one prime factor.
- Numbers that are in the same connected component (connected through shared prime factors) can be rearranged among themselves.
- Numbers in different connected components cannot be swapped with each other.

Therefore, for a sequence to be coprime-sortable:
- The relative ordering of numbers from different connected components must already match the target sorted order.
- In other words, if we group numbers by their connected components (via shared prime factors), then within each component, we can rearrange freely, but numbers from different components cannot be swapped.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all prime factors of each number in the sequence.
2. Build a graph where numbers are nodes and edges exist between numbers that share a prime factor.
3. Find connected components in this graph.
4. For each connected component, check if the numbers in that component can be rearranged to their correct positions in the sorted array.

Wait, let me refine this. Actually, we don't need to build the full graph. The key insight is:

**A sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if within each connected component (defined by shared prime factors), the multiset of values in the original array matches the multiset of values in the sorted array.**

But actually, that's not quite right either. Let me think more clearly:

Since we can rearrange elements within a connected component freely, the elements that belong to the same connected component must be "assigned" to positions that have the same "component signature" in the sorted array.

More precisely:
- Find connected components based on shared prime factors.
- For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, each connected component must contain exactly the same multiset of values in the original sequence as it does in the final sorted sequence.

## Correct Approach

1. Find all prime factors for each number.
2. Group numbers by their prime factors (connected components).
3. For each connected component (group of numbers that can be rearranged among themselves), count the frequency of each value in the original array and in the sorted array.
4. If the frequency of each value in a component matches in both the original and sorted arrays, then it's coprime-sortable.

Actually, let me think of it differently:

Since we can rearrange elements within a connected component freely, the relative ordering of elements from different components is fixed. But we can rearrange within components.

So: a sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if for each connected component, the multiset of values in the original array exactly matches the multiset of values that should be in that component's positions in the sorted array.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Find all prime factors of each number
2. Group indices by their prime factors (using union-find or graph connectivity)
3. For each connected component, extract the values in original and sorted positions
4. Compare multisets

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors of each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Group indices by the prime factors they share
    # We'll use a set of prime factors for each value
    prime_to_indices = {}
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        for factor in factors:
            if factor not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[factor] = []
            prime_to_indices[factor].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using union-find or just group by common primes
    # Actually, we can use the fact that if two indices share a prime factor, they're in the same component
    
    # Let's build a graph and find connected components
    from collections import defaultdict, deque
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(set)
    for factor, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        for i in range(len(indices) - 1):
            graph[indices[i]].add(indices[i + 1])
    
    # This is still not right. Let me simplify.
    
    # Actually, a simpler insight: two elements are in the same component if
    # they share a common prime factor. So we can just group indices by their prime factors.
    
    # We'll create a mapping from each prime factor to the set of indices that have it
    indices_by_prime = defaultdict(set)
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        for factor in factors:
            indices_by_prime[factor].add(i)
    
    # Now find connected components: indices that are connected through shared primes
    components = []
    visited = set()
    
    # Each component is a set of indices that can be rearranged
    for i in range(n):
        if i not in visited:
            # BFS to find all indices connected to i
            component = set()
            queue = deque([i])
            visited.add(i)
            
            while queue:
                curr = queue.popleft()
                component.add(curr)
                
                # Check all primes that this index has
                factors = get_prime_factors(arr[curr])
                for factor in factors:
                    # Add all indices that have this prime factor
                    for idx in indices_by_prime[factor]:
                        if idx not in visited:
                            visited.add(idx)
                            queue.append(idx)
            
            components.append(component)
    
    # Now, for each component, check if the multiset of values in original array
    # matches the multiset of values in the sorted array for those positions
    arr_sorted = sorted(arr)
    
    for component in components:
        # Get values in original array at positions in component
        original_vals = [arr[i] for i in component]
        # Get values in sorted array at positions in component
        sorted_vals = [arr_sorted[i] for i in component]
        
        # Check if multisets match
        if sorted(original_vals) != sorted(sorted_vals):
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with my component detection. Let me reconsider.

Actually, the component detection approach above might be overcomplicating it. The key insight is:

For coprime sortability: If we can rearrange elements freely within connected components, then for each connected component, the values that appear in that component in the original array must be the same values (multiset) as in the sorted array.

But I need to ensure the component detection is correct.

Let me implement a cleaner version:

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Build mapping from prime factors to indices that have them
    prime_to_indices = {}
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        for factor in factors:
            if factor not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[factor] = []
            prime_to_indices[factor].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using union-find for efficiency
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect indices that share a prime factor
    for factor, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        # For each prime factor, connect all indices that have this factor
        for i in range(len(indices) - 1):
            union(indices[i], indices[i + 1])
    
    # Group indices by their root parent
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Check if each component has the same multiset in original and sorted arrays
    arr_sorted = sorted(arr)
    
    for component in components.values():
        original_vals = [arr[i] for i in component]
        sorted_vals = [arr_sorted[i] for i in component]
        
        if sorted(original_vals) != sorted(sorted_vals):
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1

7

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. We can only swap two elements if they share at least one prime factor.
3. We need to output 1 if coprime-sortable, 0 otherwise.

## Key Insights

1. If two numbers share a common prime factor, they can be swapped with each other.
2. This means numbers that have a common prime factor can be freely rearranged among each other.
3. The key insight is that numbers can be grouped by their prime factors - numbers that belong to the same "prime component" can be permuted freely.

## Analysis

Let me think about when sorting is impossible:

- If we have numbers that don't share any common prime factors, they form separate "components".
- Within each connected component (where elements share at least one prime factor), we can rearrange elements freely.
- For the sequence to be sortable, each element must be able to reach its final position in the sorted array.

The critical observation: **Two numbers can be swapped if they share a prime factor. This creates connected components based on prime factors.**

The sorting is possible if and only if for every prime factor, all numbers that have this prime factor can be rearranged among themselves.

In other words, within each maximal set of numbers that share a common prime factor (or are reachable through prime factors), the relative ordering of elements is not constrained.

3. For each group, check if the elements in that group can be rearranged to reach their correct positions in the sorted array.

The key insight from known problems like this: if two elements share a prime factor, they belong to the same equivalence class. We can freely permute elements within each equivalence class.

Therefore, the sorting is possible if and only if for each equivalence class (defined by shared prime factors), the multiset of values in the original array for that class is the same as the multiset of values in the sorted array for the positions that correspond to that class.

But since the values are only constrained by their prime factors, and we can freely permute within classes, we just need to check that each value in the original array can be matched to a value in the sorted array that has the same prime factors (or can reach it through the component).

Actually, let me implement a simpler approach:

1. For each number, get its prime factors
2. Build a graph of indices (positions) where two indices are connected if the numbers at those positions share a prime factor
3. Find connected components
4. For each component, check if the multiset of values in the original array for that component can be rearranged to match the values that should be in those positions in the sorted array

Since we can permute freely within each component, we just need to check that the multiset of values in each component matches the multiset of values in the sorted array for those positions.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors of each number
    def prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Build graph of indices connected by shared prime factors
    from collections import defaultdict, deque
    
    # Find all prime factors for each number
    prime_factors_list = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors_list.append(prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build adjacency list for indices
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # If they share a common prime factor
            if prime_factors_list[i] & prime_factors_list[j]:  # intersection is non-empty
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using BFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            component = []
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                component.append(node)
                
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        queue.append(neighbor)
            
            components.append(component)
    
    # Get sorted version of the array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values in original positions matches 
    # the multiset of values in the corresponding sorted positions
    for component in components:
        # Get values in original array for this component
        original_values = [a[i] for i in component]
        # Get values in sorted array for these component positions
        # We need to map each position in the component to its sorted value
        component_positions = [i for i in component]
        sorted_values_at_positions = [sorted_a[pos] for pos in component_positions]
        
        # Check if the multiset of original values can be rearranged to match sorted values
        from collections import Counter
        original_counter = Counter(original_values)
        sorted_counter = Counter(sorted_values_at_positions)
        
        if original_counter != sorted_counter:
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

Wait, I think I made a mistake. The component positions should map to their final sorted values, but I'm not correctly aligning the positions.

Actually, I need to be more careful. For each connected component, the values in those positions can be freely rearranged. So when we sort the array, the values that end up in those positions must be exactly the values that were originally in those positions (up to permutation).

So I should gather all the values in the original array at positions in the component, and compare their multiset to the multiset of values that should be in those positions after sorting.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors of each number
    def prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Build graph of indices connected by shared prime factors
    from collections import defaultdict, deque
    
    # Find all prime factors for each number
    prime_factors_list = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors_list.append(prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build adjacency list for indices
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # If they share a common prime factor
            if prime_factors_list[i] & prime_factors_list[j]:  # intersection is non-empty
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using BFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            component = []
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                component.append(node)
                
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        queue.append(neighbor)
            
            components.append(component)
    
    # Get sorted version of the array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values in original positions 
    # can be rearranged to match the values in the sorted array at those positions
    for component in components:
        # Get the original values at positions in this component
        original_values = [a[i] for i in component]
        # Get the values that should be at these positions in the sorted array
        sorted_values_at_component_positions = [sorted_a[i] for i in component]
        
        from collections import Counter
        original_counter = Counter(original_values)
        sorted_counter = Counter(sorted_values_at_component_positions)
        
        if original_counter != sorted_counter:
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)
```

    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors of each number

                x //= d
            d += 1

        prime_factors_list.append(prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build adjacency list for indices
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(n):

            # If they share a common prime factor
            if prime_factors_list[i] & prime_factors_list[j]:  # intersection is non-empty
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)

    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for i in range(n):

            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:

                component.append(node)
                
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        queue.append(neighbor)
            
            components.append(component)
    
    # Get sorted version of the array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)

    # can be rearranged to match the values in the sorted array at those positions
