I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. We can only swap two elements if they share at least one prime factor.
3. We need to output 1 if coprime-sortable, 0 otherwise.

## Key Insight

This is essentially about connected components in a graph where:
- Each number is a node
- Two numbers are connected if they share a prime factor
- Elements within the same connected component can be freely rearranged among themselves
- For the sequence to be sortable, each element must be able to reach its correct position in the sorted array

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand what makes sorting possible
- Elements that can reach each other through swaps must be in the same connected component
- In the sorted array, each element has a target position
- For sorting to be possible, all elements that should end up in a position must be able to reach that position through swaps

### Step 2: Find connected components
- Build a graph where numbers are nodes and edges exist between numbers sharing prime factors
- Use union-find or DFS to find connected components

### Step 3: Check if sorting is possible
- For each position in the sorted array, check if the element that should be there can reach that position
- Since elements within the same connected component can be freely rearranged, we just need to ensure that the element at each position in the original array belongs to the same connected component as the element that should be at that position in the sorted array

### Step 4: Implementation approach
1. Find all prime factors of each number
2. Group numbers by their prime factors (using union-find)
3. For each number, determine which connected component it belongs to
4. Create the sorted array and check if elements in each connected component are consistent

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find all prime factors for each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Get prime factors for all numbers
    prime_factors = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Use union-find to group numbers that share prime factors
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each prime factor, union all numbers that have this prime factor
    # First, collect all primes that appear
    all_primes = set()
    for i in range(n):
        all_primes.update(prime_factors[i])
    
    # For each prime, union all numbers that have this prime factor
    for prime in all_primes:
        prime_indices = []
        for i in range(n):
            if prime in prime_factors[i]:
                prime_indices.append(i)
        
        # Union all indices that have this prime factor
        for i in range(1, len(prime_indices)):
            union(prime_indices[0], prime_indices[i])
    
    # Create the sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Check if each element in the original array can reach its sorted position
    # Elements can only move within their connected component
    # So for each position i, original_a[i] and sorted_a[i] must be in the same component
    
    for i in range(n):
        original_val = a[i]
        target_val = sorted_a[i]
        
        # Find the component of original_val
        original_component = find(i)
        
        # Find the component of target_val
        # We need to find j such that a[j] = target_val and then find its component
        # But we don't have the value-to-index mapping
        
        # Instead, let's create a mapping: for each value, what are the indices that have it?
        # Actually, we need to be more careful
        
        # We should check if the value at position i in original array belongs to the same component
        # as the value that should be at position i in sorted array
        
        # The value at position i in original array is a[i]
        # The value that should be at position i in sorted array is sorted_a[i]
        
        # We need to find the positions where sorted_a[i] appears in original array
        # Actually, let's just check the component of a[i] and the component of the value
        # that should be at position i
        
        # Find which original position has the value sorted_a[i]
        val_positions = {}
        for j in range(n):
            if a[j] not in val_positions:
                val_positions[a[j]] = []
            val_positions[a[j]].append(j)
        
        # For each value in sorted_a, find its component
        comp_for_sorted_val = {}
        
        # For each value, find the component of the first position where it appears
        # All occurrences of the same value will be in the same component?
        # Actually the component is determined by the prime factors of the value
        
        # Let's do this differently: for each value, determine which component it belongs to
        
        # Create a mapping from value to component (using the position of first occurrence)
        val_to_comp = {}
        
        # We need to find the component of each unique value
        # For a value v, find any position j where a[j] = v, then its component is find(j)
        for j in range(n):
            val = a[j]
            if val not in val_to_comp:
                val_to_comp[val] = find(j)
        
        # Check if original value at position i has the same component as the sorted value at position i
        original_val = a[i]
        target_val = sorted_a[i]
        
        if original_val not in val_to_comp:
            # This shouldn't happen since original_val is in the array
            return 0
            
        if target_val not in val_to_comp:
            return 0
            
        if val_to_comp[original_val] != val_to_comp[target_val]:
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with my approach. Let me reconsider.

The problem is that multiple positions can have the same value, and they might not all be in the same component. However, if two elements have the same value, they have the same prime factors, so they should be in the same component (since they share all prime factors of that value).

Actually, let me simplify:

1. Two numbers are in the same component if they share a prime factor
2. For sorting to work, each element must be able to reach its target position
3. This means that for each position i, the element originally at position i must be able to reach the element that should be at position i in the sorted array

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors for each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Union-find data structure
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Get prime factors for each element
    prime_factors_list = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors_list.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # For each prime factor, union all indices that have this prime factor
    all_primes = set()
    for factors in prime_factors_list:
        all_primes.update(factors)
    
    # Union indices that share prime factors
    for prime in all_primes:
        indices_with_prime = []
        for i in range(n):
            if prime in prime_factors_list[i]:
                indices_with_prime.append(i)
        
        # Union all indices that have this prime factor
        for j in range(1, len(indices_with_prime)):
            union(indices_with_prime[0], indices_with_prime[j])
    
    # Create sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Check if the sequence can be sorted
    # For each position i, the original element a[i] and the target element sorted_a[i]
    # must be in the same connected component
    
    # First, create a mapping from value to component (using first occurrence)
    value_to_component = {}
    for i in range(n):
        val = a[i]
        if val not in value_to_component:
            value_to_component[val] = find(i)
    
    # Check each position
    for i in range(n):
        original_val = a[i]
        target_val = sorted_a[i]
        
        # If the target value doesn't exist in the array, this is impossible
        # But since we sorted, it should exist
        if target_val not in value_to_component:
            return 0
            
        # The original value at position i should be in the same component as 
        # the target value that should be at position i
        if value_to_component[original_val] != value_to_component[target_val]:
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. Two elements can be swapped if they share at least one prime factor.
3. We need to output 1 if coprime-sortable, 0 otherwise.

## Key Insight

This is essentially a graph connectivity problem:
- Each number in the array is a node
- Two nodes are connected if their numbers share a common prime factor
- We can perform swaps within connected components
- Therefore, elements in the same connected component can be rearranged among themselves
- For the sequence to be sortable, each connected component must contain all elements that should end up in that component when sorted

## Algorithm Steps

1. **Find prime factors** of each number
2. **Group numbers by their prime factors** - numbers sharing prime factors are in the same component
3. **Identify connected components** using union-find or graph connectivity
4. **Check if each connected component contains exactly the elements that should be in that position after sorting**

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we can perform arbitrary swaps within connected components, elements in the same connected component can be rearranged in any order. But elements from different components cannot be swapped.

So the condition is:
- After sorting, elements that belong to the same connected component must end up in the positions that are already occupied by elements from that component in the sorted array.

Actually, let's think more carefully:
- In the sorted array, elements are in their correct positions based on value
- For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, elements that belong to the same connected component (sharing a common prime factor) must be able to reach their final positions through swaps
- Since we can rearrange elements within a connected component, the constraint is that the set of elements in each connected component must match the set of elements that occupy those positions in the sorted array

## Correct Approach

1. **Find connected components** by grouping numbers that share prime factors
2. **Create the target sorted array**
3. **For each connected component, check if the elements in that component in the original array can be rearranged to match the elements in that component in the sorted array**

Actually, since we can rearrange elements within each connected component freely, the necessary and sufficient condition is that for each connected component, the multiset of elements in that component in the original array must be the same as the multiset of elements in that component in the sorted array.

But here's the key insight: we need to group elements by their prime factors, and check if the relative ordering of elements that belong to different components is preserved in a way that allows sorting.

Wait, let me reframe:

Since swapping is only allowed between elements sharing a prime factor, elements can only move within their connected component. Therefore:
- The relative positions of elements from different connected components cannot change
- But within each component, elements can be rearranged

So the sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if for every connected component, the multiset of elements in that component is preserved (i.e., the same elements appear in both original and sorted arrays).

## Implementation Steps

1. For each number, find its prime factors
2. Group numbers by their prime factors (all numbers sharing at least one prime factor are connected)
3. For each connected component, collect the elements
4. Sort the original array and compare the elements in each component to see if they match

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors of each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Group numbers by their prime factors
    # Each number belongs to components defined by its prime factors
    prime_to_numbers = {}
    
    for i, num in enumerate(a):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        for factor in factors:
            if factor not in prime_to_numbers:
                prime_to_numbers[factor] = []
            prime_to_numbers[factor].append((num, i))
    
    # Find connected components - each component is defined by union of all numbers that share any prime factor
    # We can use union-find or just group by the prime factor connections
    
    # Create a mapping from each number's prime factors to its component
    component_of = {}
    
    # Group by prime factors and create component identifiers
    prime_components = {}
    component_id = 0
    
    for prime, numbers in prime_to_numbers.items():
        # All numbers with this prime factor are in the same component
        # But we need to find the union of all components
        # Actually, we can just use a set of numbers that share any prime factor
        pass
    
    # Alternative approach: create components directly
    # Two numbers are in the same component if they share at least one prime factor
    
    # Create a graph where each number is a node and edges exist between numbers sharing prime factors
    # Use union-find to find connected components
    
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each pair of numbers, if they share a prime factor, union them
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if a[i] and a[j] share any prime factor
            factors_i = get_prime_factors(a[i])
            factors_j = get_prime_factors(a[j])
            
            if factors_i & factors_j:  # if intersection is not empty
                union(i, j)
    
    # Now group indices by their component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Get the sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values in original and sorted array matches
    # The component contains indices, and we need to check if the values at these indices in original
    # can be rearranged to become the values at these indices in sorted array
    
    # For each component, get the values in original and in sorted array for those indices
    for component_indices in components.values():
        original_values = [a[i] for i in component_indices]
        sorted_values = [sorted_a[i] for i in component_indices]
        
        # Check if both multisets are equal
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

Wait, this approach might be too slow (O(N²) for checking prime factors between all pairs). Let me optimize.

Actually, we don't need to check all pairs. We can use the fact that we can find connected components efficiently.

```python
def get_prime_factors(x):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= x:
        while x % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            x //= d
        d += 1
    if x > 1:
        factors.add(x)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n <= 1:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Union-Find for connected components
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each number, get its prime factors
    prime_factors = []
    for num in a:
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        prime_factors.append(factors)
    
    # Check if any two numbers share a common prime factor
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if a[i] and a[j] share a common prime factor
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection is not empty
                union(i, j)
    
    # Group indices by component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Get the sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Check if each component's multiset matches in original and sorted arrays
    for component_indices in components.values():
        original_values = [a[i] for i in component_indices]
        sorted_values = [sorted_a[i] for i in component_indices]
        
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. Two elements can be swapped if they share at least one prime factor.
3. We need to output 1 if coprime-sortable, 0 otherwise.

## Key Insight

The key insight is that elements that can be swapped form connected components. Elements in the same connected component can be rearranged freely among themselves because:
- If two elements share a prime factor, they can be swapped
- Through a series of swaps, any two elements within the same component can eventually be exchanged

So the question becomes: can we achieve the sorted order by only rearranging elements within their connected components?

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Find prime factors of each number**: For each element, find all its prime factors.

2. **Group elements by prime factors**: Elements that share at least one prime factor belong to the same connected component.

3. **Check if sorting is possible**: For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, when we sort the array, each element should remain within its original connected component.

4. **Implementation approach**: 
   - Build a graph where elements are nodes and edges exist between elements that share a prime factor
   - Find connected components using DFS or Union-Find
   - For each connected component, check if the sorted elements in that component match the elements in the original component

## Detailed Algorithm

1. Find prime factorization for each number
2. Use Union-Find to group elements that share prime factors
3. For each connected component, collect the elements
4. Sort the array and check if each element in the original sequence belongs to the same component as the corresponding element in the sorted sequence

```python
def prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def find_root(parent, x):
    if parent[x] != x:
        parent[x] = find_root(parent, parent[x])
    return parent[x]

def union(parent, rank, x, y):
    rx, ry = find_root(parent, x), find_root(parent, y)
    if rx == ry:
        return
    if rank[rx] < rank[ry]:
        rx, ry = ry, rx
    parent[ry] = rx
    if rank[rx] == rank[ry]:
        rank[rx] += 1

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create prime factor sets for each element
    prime_factors_list = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors_list.append(prime_factors(num))
    
    # Use Union-Find to group elements by shared prime factors
    parent = list(range(n))
    rank = [0] * n
    
    # For each prime factor, union all elements that have this prime factor
    # We need to find all unique prime factors first
    primes = set()
    for factors in prime_factors_list:
        primes.update(factors)
    
    # For each prime factor, union all elements that contain it
    for prime in primes:
        for i in range(n):
            if prime in prime_factors_list[i]:
                # Find which indices have this prime factor
                for j in range(i + 1, n):
                    if prime in prime_factors_list[j]:
                        union(parent, rank, i, j)
    
    # Group elements by their root (connected component)
    component = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find_root(parent, i)
        if root not in component:
            component[root] = []
        component[root].append(i)
    
    # Create sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Check if each element in sorted array belongs to the same component as the original
    for i in range(n):
        original_idx = i
        sorted_idx = sorted_a.index(a[i])  # This won't work as we need position
        
        # Better approach: For each position i in original array, check if it belongs to the same component as position i in sorted array
        # Actually, we need to match the sorted sequence with original sequence component-wise
        
        # Let's reframe: For each component, the elements in that component should be exactly the elements that appear in that component in the sorted sequence
        pass
    
    # Correct approach: For each connected component, collect the elements in their original positions
    # Then sort them and check if they match the values that should be in those positions
    
    # Create a mapping: for each component, what are the original values at positions in that component
    component_values = {}
    for root in component:
        values = []
        for pos in component[root]:
            values.append(a[pos])
        component_values[root] = sorted(values)
    
    # Now, for each component, check if the sorted sequence has the same values in the same positions
    # We need to assign positions to components
    
    # Actually, simpler: we can check if the multiset of values in each component matches
    # But we need to ensure the relative order can be achieved
    
    # Final approach: Check if we can achieve the sorted sequence by only rearranging within components
    # For each component, the elements in that component must be exactly the elements that should end up in those positions after sorting
    
    # Create positions for sorted array
    sorted_positions = sorted(range(n), key=lambda i: a[i])
    
    # Create component assignment for original positions
    comp_assignment = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find_root(parent, i)
        comp_assignment[i] = root
    
    # For each connected component, get the positions and values
    for root in component:
        positions = component[root]
        original_values = [a[pos] for pos in positions]
        sorted_values = [sorted_a[pos] for pos in positions]
        
        # The original values in this component must be exactly the values that should be in these positions
        # So we need to check if sorted_values contains exactly the same elements as original_values
        if sorted(set(original_values)) != sorted(set(sorted_values)):
            print(0)
            return
    
    # If we reach here, it's possible to sort
    print(1)

solve()
```

## Simplified Correct Approach

```python
def prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def find_root(parent, x):
    if parent[x] != x:
        parent[x] = find_root(parent, parent[x])
    return parent[x]

def union(parent, rank, x, y):
    rx, ry = find_root(parent, x), find_root(parent, y)
    if rx == ry:
        return
    if rank[rx] < rank[ry]:
        rx, ry = ry, rx
    parent[ry] = rx
    if rank[rx] == rank[ry]:
        rank[rx] += 1

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_factors_list = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors_list.append(prime_factors(num))
    
    # Use Union-Find to group elements by shared prime factors
    parent = list(range(n))
    rank = [0] * n
    
    # For each prime factor, union all elements that have it
    all_primes = set()
    for factors in prime_factors_list:
        all_primes.update(factors)
    
    for prime in all_primes:
        for i in range(n):
            if prime in prime_factors_list[i]:
                for j in range(i + 1, n):
                    if prime in prime_factors_list[j]:
                        union(parent, rank, i, j)
    
    # Group elements by component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find_root(parent, i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Create sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # For each component, check if the values in that component can be rearranged to match the sorted values
    # We need to check that the multiset of values in each component matches the multiset of values that would be in those positions after sorting
    
    # For each component, get the original values and the target values (sorted array)
    for root in components:
        positions = components[root]
        original_values = [a[pos] for pos in positions]
        sorted_values_at_positions = [sorted_a[pos] for pos in positions]
        
        # The values in the component must be the same as the values that would be at those positions after sorting
        # So we need to check if the multiset of original_values matches the multiset of sorted_values_at_positions
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values_at_positions):
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Examples

5
6 4 2 3 7

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of numbers and want to sort it in increasing order
2. We can only swap two elements if they have a common prime factor
3. We need to determine if the sequence can be sorted using these swaps

## Key Insights

This is essentially about connectivity in a graph where:
- Each number is a node
- Two numbers are connected if they share a common prime factor
- We can swap elements within the same connected component

For the sequence to be coprime-sortable:
- All elements that need to end up in each position must be able to reach each other through swaps
- This means elements in the same connected component (connected by shared prime factors) can be rearranged among themselves
- Elements in different connected components cannot be swapped with each other

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all prime factors of all numbers in the sequence
2. Group numbers by their prime factors - numbers that share at least one prime factor are in the same component
3. For each connected component, check if the elements in that component can be rearranged to their correct positions in the sorted sequence
4. The key insight: if we can reach the final sorted arrangement, then within each connected component, the multiset of elements must match the elements that should go into that component in the sorted sequence

## Detailed Approach

1. Find the primes and group numbers by their prime factors
2. Build connected components based on shared primes
3. For each component, get the elements that belong to it in the original sequence
4. In the sorted sequence, get which elements belong to each component
5. Check if the multiset of elements in each component matches in original vs sorted sequences

## Implementation

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Get prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build a mapping from prime to elements that have that prime factor
    prime_to_elements = {}
    for i, prime_set in enumerate(prime_factors):
        for prime in prime_set:
            if prime not in prime_to_elements:
                prime_to_elements[prime] = []
            prime_to_elements[prime].append(i)
    
    # For each prime, we get the set of indices that have that prime factor
    # This helps us identify connected components
    
    # Build a union-find or simply check connectivity
    # Instead, we'll check if elements in each connected component can be rearranged
    
    # Create a component for each element - two elements are in same component if they share a prime
    # We'll group indices by their prime factors - any two indices that share a prime are connected
    
    # Find all connected components using union-find or by grouping
    components = []
    visited = [False] * n
    
    def find_component(i):
        if visited[i]:
            return []
        component = [i]
        visited[i] = True
        
        # Find all elements connected through shared primes
        primes = prime_factors[i]
        for prime in primes:
            if prime in prime_to_elements:
                for j in prime_to_elements[prime]:
                    if not visited[j]:
                        component.extend(find_component(j))
        return component
    
    # Actually, simpler approach: find all components using union-find or just check each connection
    # Alternative: for each element, find all elements it can reach through shared primes
    
    # Let's do it differently: find all connected components
    index_to_component = {}
    comp_id = 0
    
    # Create graph where edges exist between indices that share prime factors
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if they share any prime factor
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection exists
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using DFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            component = []
            stack = [i]
            visited[i] = True
            while stack:
                node = stack.pop()
                component.append(node)
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        stack.append(neighbor)
            components.append(component)
    
    # Check if sorting is possible
    # For each component, the elements in the original array and the sorted array
    # should have the same multiset of values
    
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values in original and sorted are the same
    for component in components:
        original_values = [arr[i] for i in component]
        sorted_values = [sorted_arr[i] for i in component]
        
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            return "0"
    
    return "1"

print(solve())
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

The key insight is that we can only swap two numbers if they share a common prime factor. We need to determine if we can sort the sequence in increasing order using only such swaps.

## Key Observations

1. If two numbers share a common prime factor, they can be swapped with each other.

2. Numbers that share at least one prime factor can be connected through chains of swaps.

3. If all numbers in the sequence belong to different "prime component" groups, then numbers from different groups cannot be swapped.

4. For the sequence to be sortable, we need to be able to rearrange all elements to their correct positions in increasing order.

## Core Insight

The critical insight is that numbers that share a common prime factor can be freely rearranged among themselves. This means:

- If we can group numbers by their prime factors, then within each group that has at least one prime factor in common with another number in the sequence, the numbers in that group can be freely permuted.

- For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, we need that for each prime factor, all the numbers that contain that prime factor can be freely rearranged.

- However, there's a catch: if we have a prime factor that appears in multiple positions, those numbers can be permuted among themselves.

## Algorithm

The key insight from number theory and graph theory:
- Numbers can be connected through their prime factors.
- We can think of this as a graph where each number is a node, and there's an edge between two numbers if they share a common prime factor.
- The connected components of this graph can be freely permuted within themselves.
- For coprime sorting to work, we need that for each prime factor, the positions of numbers containing that prime factor can be rearranged.

Actually, the simpler view: 
- Two numbers can be swapped if they share a common prime factor.
- This means that all numbers that share at least one common prime factor can be freely rearranged among each other.
- For the sequence to be sortable, the relative ordering of numbers that don't share any prime factors must not create any dependency.

3. If for every prime factor, the numbers containing it can be rearranged into their sorted positions, then it's possible.

After thinking about it, I believe the answer is **1** if and only if there exists at least one prime factor that appears in multiple numbers in the sequence, or more precisely, if we can connect all necessary elements through prime factors.

Actually, the correct insight from similar problems: 

The sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if all numbers can be partitioned such that within each partition (defined by shared prime factors), the elements can be freely rearranged to their correct positions in the sorted array.

But since we're only allowed swaps between numbers with common prime factors, two positions can be swapped only if the numbers at those positions share a common prime factor.

Therefore, positions can be connected if the values at those positions share a prime factor.

The algorithm:
1. For each prime factor p, create a set of indices where numbers contain p.
2. The sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if for every position i in the sorted array, the number at that position shares a prime factor with the number at some other position, allowing the necessary swaps.

Actually, the fundamental insight from graph theory: 
- Create a graph where each number is a node.
- Connect nodes if they share a common prime factor.
- The graph must be connected (or more precisely, the components must allow the final permutation).

But we don't need the entire graph to be connected - we just need that the permutation we want can be achieved through the allowed swaps.

After research in my knowledge base, I recall that for "coprime sorting" to work, the necessary and sufficient condition is that there exists at least one prime number that divides at least two of the numbers in the sequence. But this isn't sufficient.

Actually, let me implement a simpler solution:

1. Generate all prime factors for each number.
2. For each prime factor, collect all positions where the number has that prime factor.
3. The sequence is coprime-sortable if for each prime factor, the numbers containing that prime factor can be rearranged into their correct sorted positions.

But I think the actual answer is: the sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if there is no "independent component" of numbers that cannot be connected through prime factors.

The simplest approach: if all numbers are pairwise coprime (no two share a prime factor), then no swaps are possible, so sorting cannot happen unless the sequence is already sorted.

But if there's any shared prime factor, we can swap those numbers.

Actually, the correct insight: 
- The sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if in the sorted version, there exists a prime factor that appears in at least two different numbers, and those numbers can be connected through swaps.

```python
def sieve(n):
    is_prime = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_prime[0] = is_prime[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n**0.5) + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            for j in range(i*i, n + 1, i):
                is_prime[j] = False
    primes = []
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            primes.append(i)
    return primes, is_prime

def get_prime_factors(n, primes):
    factors = []
    for p in primes:
        if p * p > n:
            break
        while n % p == 0:
            factors.append(p)
            n //= p
    if n > 1:
        factors.append(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Get all primes up to max(arr)
    max_val = max(arr) if arr else 0
    if max_val == 0:
        print(1)
        return
        
    primes, is_prime = sieve(max_val)
    
    # For each position, get its prime factors
    prime_to_positions = {}
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num, primes)
        for factor in factors:
            if factor not in prime_to_positions:
                prime_to_positions[factor] = []
            prime_to_positions[factor].append(i)

    # After research, I recall that the correct solution is:
    # The sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if there exists a prime factor that appears in at least one number of the sequence, and that the values can be rearranged.
    
    # But in known problems like this, the answer is 0 if all values are pairwise coprime.
    
    # In example 2, values are not pairwise coprime.
    
    # Perhaps the answer is always 1 if there is at least one shared prime factor.
    
    # Let me implement that and see.
    
    # If there is any shared prime factor among any two numbers, then we can swap them.
    # Since we can swap in any order, we can achieve any permutation of numbers that are connected through shared prime factors.
    
    # In a connected component (connected by shared prime factors), we can freely permute the values.
    
    # For the sorted array to be achievable, the values in each connected component must be exactly the values that belong to that component in the sorted array.
    
    # But we can achieve any permutation within a connected component.
    
    # So the condition is: if the graph is connected or if the components allow the sorted permutation.
    
    # Actually, since we can permute within a connected component, we can achieve any arrangement of values within a component, as long as the component values are the same.
    
    # Therefore, the sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if for every connected component of the graph (where two numbers are connected if they share a common prime factor), the values in that component can be moved to the desired sorted positions.
    
    # But we can always achieve the sorted order within a component if the values are the same.
    
    # The only issue is if there's a value that is "trapped" in a component but needs to go to a different component.
    
    # Actually, the values are fixed - each value stays with its prime factors.
    
    # Since we can swap within components, we can achieve any permutation of values within a component.
    
    # Therefore, the sorted array can be achieved if the values in each component are exactly the values that should be in that component in the sorted array.
    
    # Since the sorted array is determined, and we can permute values within components, we can achieve it.
    
    # But wait - we have to ensure the values are correct.
    
    # The only constraint is that a value can only move to a position that is in the same connected component.
    
    # So if in the sorted array, a value from component C appears in a position not in component C, it's impossible.
    
    # This is the key!
    
    # Algorithm:
    # 1. For each prime factor, collect all positions where a number has that factor.
    # 2. The positions form a graph where an edge exists between positions if they share a common prime factor.
    # 3. Find connected components of this graph.
    # 4. For each connected component, get the values in those positions in the original array.
    # 5. In the sorted array, for each position in the component, we need to have a value that is in the set of values from the original component.
    # 6. More precisely: the values from the original component must be exactly the values that appear in the sorted array at positions in this component.
    
    # But since we can permute freely within the component, we just need to check that the multiset of values in the original component matches the multiset of values in the sorted array for the positions in that component.
    
    # Steps:
    # - Create a graph where positions are nodes, and two positions are connected if the values at those positions share a common prime factor.
    # - Find connected components.
    # - For each component, compare the multiset of values in original array at positions in component with the multiset of values in sorted array at positions in component.
    
    # If all components match, return 1, else 0.
    
    # This should work.
    
    # However, the graph has up to 10^5 nodes and we need to build it. We can optimize by using prime factors to group positions.
    
    # Plan:
    # 1. For each prime factor, collect all positions where the number has that factor.
    # 2. Use union-find to connect positions that share a common prime factor.
    # 3. For each connected component, check if the values match in sorted array.
    
    # Implementation:
    
    # Build the graph using union-find
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each prime factor, union all positions that have that factor
    # But we need to get all prime factors first
    
    # Get prime factors for each value
    prime_to_positions = {}
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num, primes)
        for factor in factors:
            if factor not in prime_to_positions:
                prime_to_positions[factor] = []
            prime_to_positions[factor].append(i)
    
    # For each prime factor, union all positions in that factor group
    for factor, positions in prime_to_positions.items():
        for i in range(1, len(positions)):
            union(positions[0], positions[i])
    
    # Now group positions by component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Create sorted array
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values in the original array at component positions matches the multiset of values in the sorted array at those positions
    for root, positions in components.items():
        orig_values = [arr[pos] for pos in positions]
        sorted_values = [sorted_arr[pos] for pos in positions]
        
        if sorted(orig_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be careful - in the sorted array, the values are assigned to positions. But we need to check if the values that can move to the component positions in the sorted array are the same.

Actually, my approach is correct: within each connected component, we can freely permute the values, so we need the multiset of original values in the component to match the multiset of sorted values in the component positions.

```python
def sieve(n):
    is_prime = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_prime[0] = is_prime[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n**0.5) + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            for j in range(i*i, n + 1, i):
                is_prime[j] = False
    primes = []
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            primes.append(i)
    return primes, is_prime

def get_prime_factors(n, primes):
    factors = []
    for p in primes:
        if p * p > n:
            break
        while n % p == 0:
            factors.append(p)
            n //= p
    if n > 1:
        factors.append(n)
    return factors

n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

if n == 0:
    print(1)
else:
    max_val = max(arr)
    if max_val == 0:
        print(1)
    else:
        primes, _ = sieve(max_val)
        
        # For each position, get its prime factors
        prime_to_positions = {}
        
        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            factors = get_prime_factors(num, primes)
            for factor in factors:
                if factor not in prime_to_positions:
                    prime_to_positions[factor] = []
                prime_to_positions[factor].append(i)
        
        # Union find to connect positions with common prime factors
        parent = list(range(n))
        
        def find(x):
            if parent[x] != x:
                parent[x] = find(parent[x])
            return parent[x]
        
        def union(x, y):
            px, py = find(x), find(y)
            if px != py:
                parent[px] = py
        
        # For each prime factor, union all positions that have it
        for factor, positions in prime_to_positions.items():
            for i in range(1, len(positions)):
                union(positions[0], positions[i])
        
        # Group positions by component
        components = {}
        for i in range(n):
            root = find(i)
            if root not in components:
                components[root] = []
            components[root].append(i)
        
        # Create sorted array
        sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
        
        # Check each component
        valid = True
        for root, positions in components.items():
            # Get values in original array at these positions
            orig_values = [arr[pos] for pos in positions]
            # Get values in sorted array at these positions
            sorted_values = [sorted_arr[pos] for pos in positions]
            
            if sorted(orig_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
                valid = False
                break
        
        print(1 if valid else 0)
```

def sieve(n):
    is_prime = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_prime[0] = is_prime[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n**0.5) + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            for j in range(i*i, n + 1, i):
                is_prime[j] = False
    primes = []
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            primes.append(i)
    return primes, is_prime

def get_prime_factors(n, primes):

        if p * p > n:
            break
        while n % p == 0:
            factors.append(p)
            n //= p
    if n > 1:
        factors.append(n)
    return factors

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

2. This means numbers that share the same prime factor can be rearranged among themselves
3. If two numbers have no common prime factor, they cannot be swapped directly
4. So we need to group numbers by their prime factors

Two numbers can be swapped if they share a prime factor. This means that all numbers that share at least one prime factor can be rearranged among themselves.

So the answer is "1" if and only if for every prime factor, the elements that have that prime factor can be rearranged to their target positions in the sorted array.

Actually, here's the key: if two numbers don't share any prime factor, they cannot be swapped directly. But if they're part of the same connected component through transitive sharing (via other numbers), they can be rearranged.

The connected components are defined by the prime factors. Two numbers are in the same component if they share a prime factor with some other number in the component.

So I need to:
1. Find all prime factors of each number
2. Connect numbers that share a prime factor
3. Find connected components
4. For each component, check if the multiset of numbers in the original array matches the multiset of numbers that should be in those positions in the sorted array

But actually, since we can swap within components freely, the elements in each component can be rearranged to any permutation. So the condition is simply that the sorted sequence can be achieved by permuting elements within each connected component.

This means: for each connected component, the elements in that component in the sorted array must be exactly the elements that were in that component in the original array.

Wait, no - the elements in the component are fixed. We just need to check if the multiset of values in the component matches in both the original and the sorted array.

So algorithm:
1. Find prime factors of each number
2. Build a graph where numbers are connected if they share a prime factor
3. Find connected components
4. For each component, get the multiset of values in the original array at positions in that component
5. For each component, get the multiset of values in the sorted array at the same positions
6. If all multisets match, return 1, else 0

But actually, since we need to assign values to positions, we need to check if the values can be rearranged.

Actually, the simpler answer is: the sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if for every prime factor, the elements that have that prime factor can be rearranged to their target positions in the sorted array.

But here's a crucial insight: if two numbers don't share a prime factor, they cannot be swapped directly. However, if they can be connected through other numbers, they might be able to be swapped.

Actually, the connected components are defined by the prime factors. Two numbers are in the same component if there's a path between them where each edge connects numbers sharing a prime factor.

So if the sorted array is achievable by permuting elements within each connected component, then yes.

So the algorithm:

1. Read input
2. Find prime factors of each number
3. Build graph: connect numbers that share a prime factor
4. Find connected components of numbers
5. For each component, take the multiset of values in original array at positions in that component
6. Take the multiset of values in sorted array at positions in that component
7. If these multisets match for all components, return 1, else 0

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create prime factor map for each number
    prime_to_indices = defaultdict(set)
    for i, num in enumerate(a):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        for prime in factors:
            prime_to_indices[prime].add(i)
    
    # Build graph and find connected components
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    
    # For each prime, connect all indices that have this prime
    for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        indices_list = list(indices)
        for i in range(len(indices_list)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(indices_list)):
                u = indices_list[i]
                v = indices_list[j]
                graph[u].append(v)
                graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Find connected components using BFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            component = []
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                component.append(node)
                
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        queue.append(neighbor)
            
            components.append(component)
    
    # Get sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values in original and sorted array match
    for component in components:
        original_values = [a[i] for i in component]
        sorted_values = [sorted_a[i] for i in component]
        
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with the graph construction. When I create the graph, I'm creating redundant edges. A better approach is to just group indices by prime factors and then use union-find or BFS on the induced graph.

Actually, my current approach is correct - for each prime factor, I connect all indices that have that prime factor. Then the connected components are the ones where numbers can be swapped.

But the graph construction has a potential issue - if there are many numbers sharing a prime factor, it might create a lot of edges, but it's still correct.

Let me optimize and simplify:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create mapping from prime to list of indices that have this prime
    prime_to_indices = defaultdict(list)
    
    for i, num in enumerate(a):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        for prime in factors:
            prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)
    
    # Build adjacency list for the graph
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    
    # For each prime, connect all indices that have this prime
    for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        indices_set = set(indices)
        for i in range(len(indices)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(indices)):
                u = indices[i]
                v = indices[j]
                graph[u].append(v)
                graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Find connected components
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            component = []
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                component.append(node)
                
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        queue.append(neighbor)
            
            components.append(component)
    
    # Check if we can achieve the sorted order
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    for component in components:
        original_vals = [a[i] for i in component]
        sorted_vals = [sorted_a[i] for i in component]
        
        if sorted(original_vals) != sorted(sorted_vals):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Chef has a box full of infinite number of identical coins. One day while playing, he made N piles each containing equal number of coins. When Chef returned home, he was angry to see that all of his piles didn't contain equal number of coins as he very strongly believes in the policy of equality for all, may it be people or piles of coins. 

In order to calm down the Chef, the assistant proposes to make all the piles equal. Chef agrees to give this task to him, but as a punishment gives him only two type of operations that he can perform.

Pick some coins from any pile and put them back in Chef's coin box.
Pick some coins from the Chef's coin box and put them on any one pile.

The first line of the input contains an integer T denoting the number of test cases.		
The first line of each test case contains a single integer N denoting the number of piles.
The second line contains N space-separated integers A_{1}, A_{2}, ..., A_{N} denoting the number of coins in each pile.
 
------ Output ------ 

For each test case, output a single line containing an integer corresponding to the minimum number of operations assistant needs to do.

------ Constraints ------ 

$1 ≤ T ≤ 10$
$1 ≤ N ≤ 10^{5}$
$1 ≤ A_{i} ≤ 10^{5}$

1. Chef has N piles of coins, each initially with some number of coins
2. The assistant can perform two operations:
   - Pick coins from any pile and put them back in the box (removes coins from a pile)
   - Pick coins from the box and put them on any one pile (adds coins to a pile)
3. Goal: Make all piles have equal number of coins with minimum operations
4. We need to find the minimum number of operations

- Remove coins from piles (operation 1)
- Add coins to piles (operation 2)

- Operations needed = sum of absolute differences from current values to T
- For each pile with value A[i], we need |A[i] - T| operations

Otherwise, it's the minimum over all possible targets of the number of piles that need to be changed.

But the number of piles that need to be changed is n minus the number of piles that already have the target value.

To minimize this, we should choose a target that appears most frequently.

In [1,2,3,4], each value appears once, so max frequency is 1.
Operations needed = 4 - 1 = 3.

In [1,1,2,2,3,3], if we choose target 1 or 2 or 3, each appears twice, so operations = 6 - 2 = 4.
If we choose target 1.5, but we need integer values, so only integers.

So the minimum operations is n - (max frequency of any value).

For the sample: n=4, max frequency = 1, so answer = 4-1 = 3. This matches.

Let me verify with another example. Suppose [1,1,2,3]:
- max frequency = 2 (value 1 appears twice)
- operations = 4 - 2 = 2
- If target = 1: piles with 2,3 need to change → 2 operations
- If target = 2: piles with 1,3 need to change → 2 operations  
- If target = 3: piles with 1,2 need to change → 2 operations
- So minimum is 2, which matches.

Therefore, the solution is:
1. Count frequency of each value
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Answer = n - max_frequency

```python
from collections import Counter

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 0:
        print(0)
    else:
        freq = Counter(arr)
        max_freq = max(freq.values())
        print(n - max_freq)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Chef has N piles of coins, each with some number of coins
2. We want to make all piles have the same number of coins
3. We have two operations:
   - Remove coins from any pile and put them back in the box
   - Add coins from the box to any one pile
4. We need to find the minimum number of operations

## Key Insight

Since we can only transfer coins between piles and the box, the total number of coins remains constant. 

To make all piles equal, we need to reach a target value for each pile. The key observation is that the final number of coins in each pile must be one of the original pile sizes (or we could choose any value, but we want minimal operations).

However, let's think about this differently: for any target value T, the number of operations needed would be:
- For piles with more than T coins: we need to remove (pile_value - T) coins each
- For piles with less than T coins: we need to add (T - pile_value) coins each

So the total operations for target T is: sum of all positive differences (since each operation moves one coin).

## Optimization

The total operations for target T is the sum of absolute differences from T: Σ|A[i] - T|.

We need to minimize this sum. This is a well-known problem - the sum of absolute deviations is minimized when T is the median of the values.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For each test case, read the array of pile sizes
2. Sort the array
3. Find the median value
4. Calculate the sum of absolute differences from the median

Let me trace through the example:
- Piles: [1, 2, 3, 4]
- Sorted: [1, 2, 3, 4]
- Median: since n=4 (even), median is between 2 and 3. Both give the same sum of absolute deviations.

Given that, I'll implement this.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    piles = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort the piles to find median
    piles.sort()
    
    # If n is odd, median is at position n//2
    # If n is even, any value between piles[n//2-1] and piles[n//2] gives same sum
    # We'll use median as piles[n//2] (this works for both cases)
    median = piles[n // 2]
    
    # Calculate total operations = sum of absolute differences from median
    total_operations = 0
    for pile in piles:
        total_operations += abs(pile - median)
    
    return total_operations

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

   - Remove coins from any pile and put them back in the box

- Remove coins from piles (operations = number of coins removed)
- Add coins to piles (operations = number of coins added)

The total number of operations needed is the sum of:
- Coins we need to remove from piles that have more than the target value
- Coins we need to add to piles that have less than the target value

## Observation

The minimum number of operations occurs when we choose a target value that minimizes the total movement of coins.

For any target value T:
- Operations = sum of (A[i] - T) for all A[i] > T + sum of (T - A[i]) for all A[i] < T
- This simplifies to: sum of |A[i] - T| for all i

So we need to find the value of T that minimizes the sum of absolute differences from each A[i].

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    piles = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort the piles to find median
    piles.sort()
    
    # For even n, the median can be any value between the two middle values
    # The sum of absolute deviations is the same for any value in this range
    # We can use the middle elements
    
    # If n is odd, median is at index n//2
    # If n is even, we can use either of the two middle elements
    # The sum will be the same
    
    # Calculate sum of absolute differences from median
    total_operations = 0
    median_idx = n // 2
    
    for i in range(n):
        total_operations += abs(piles[i] - piles[median_idx])
    
    return total_operations

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

```python
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    piles = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    piles.sort()

    # For even n, any value between the two middle elements gives the same result
    # We use the middle element (at index n//2)
    median = piles[n // 2]
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Chef has N piles of coins, initially with equal number of coins
2. The assistant can perform two operations:
   - Pick coins from any pile and put them back in the box
   - Pick coins from the box and put them on any one pile
3. Goal: Make all piles have equal number of coins with minimum operations
4. We need to find the minimum number of operations

## Key Insight

The assistant can only transfer coins between piles and the box, not create or destroy coins. This means the total number of coins remains constant.

To make all piles equal, we need to make all piles have the same number of coins. Let's say the final number of coins in each pile is `x`.

For each pile with `a[i]` coins:
- If `a[i] > x`: we need to remove `(a[i] - x)` coins (operation 1)
- If `a[i] < x`: we need to add `(x - a[i])` coins (operation 2)

The total operations needed = sum of all coins that need to be moved.

## Optimization

What value of `x` minimizes the total operations?

If we choose `x` to be any value, the total operations will be:
- Sum of all excess coins (piles with more than x)
- Sum of all deficit coins (piles with less than x)

Since the total coins is fixed, if we have excess coins in some piles, we must compensate with deficit in others.

The key insight is that the minimum operations occurs when we choose `x` as one of the existing pile sizes. This is because:
- For any value of `x`, the total operations = sum of absolute differences from x
- The sum of absolute differences is minimized when `x` is the median of the array

However, there's an even simpler observation: we can make all piles equal to any value, and the operations needed are the sum of absolute differences from that value.

- For each pile, we need to move `|a[i] - target|` coins

For an odd number of elements, median is the middle element.

- I can pick all coins from pile 3 (3 coins) and put them in the box (1 operation)
- I can pick all coins from pile 4 (4 coins) and put them in the box (1 operation)

Actually, I think I found the error. In the explanation: "if you decide to convert all the piles to contain either of 1, 2, 3, or 4 coins you will have to change the other 3 piles."

This means: if we want all piles to have value x, then we need to change each pile that doesn't have value x.

But the number of changes is N minus the count of piles that already have value x.

For x=1: 1 pile has 1, so 3 piles need to be changed → 3 operations
For x=2: 1 pile has 2, so 3 piles need to be changed → 3 operations
For x=3: 1 pile has 3, so 3 piles need to be changed → 3 operations  
For x=4: 1 pile has 4, so 3 piles need to be changed → 3 operations

And for any other value (like 2.5), we still need to change at least 4 piles? No, we have 4 piles.

Actually, we need to change the piles whose values differ from x. For each pile different from x, we need at least one operation (either add or remove).

In the case where we set target = 1, 2, 3, or 4, we need to change the other 3 piles, so 3 operations.

For any other target, say 2.5, we still need to change 4 piles (all), so 4 operations.

So the minimum is 3 operations, achieved by setting target to any of the existing values.

## Conclusion

The minimum number of operations is the minimum over all possible target values of the number of piles that need to be changed.

Since we can only make piles have values that are integers (coins are discrete), and for any target value, the number of operations is the count of piles that don't have that value.

For each possible target value (each unique value in the array), we calculate how many piles have that value, then the operations needed = N - count_of_piles_with_that_value.

We want to minimize this, which means maximizing the count of piles with the target value.

So the answer is N - (maximum frequency of any value in the array).

Let me verify:
- Array [1,2,3,4]: each value appears once, maximum frequency is 1
- Operations = 4 - 1 = 3 ✓

Another example: [1,1,2,2]
- Value 1 appears 2 times, value 2 appears 2 times
- Maximum frequency = 2
- Operations = 4 - 2 = 2

Check: 
- Target = 1: 2 piles already have 1, so 2 piles need change → 2 operations
- Target = 2: 2 piles already have 2, so 2 piles need change → 2 operations
- Answer = 2 ✓

Yes, this makes sense.

```python
from collections import Counter

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count frequency of each value
    freq = Counter(arr)
    
    # Find maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Minimum operations = n - max_freq
    return n - max_freq

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

from collections import Counter

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count frequency of each value
    freq = Counter(arr)
    
    # Find maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Minimum operations = n - max_freq
    return n - max_freq

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Chef has N piles of coins with different numbers of coins
2. The assistant can perform two operations:
   - Pick coins from any pile and put them back in the box
   - Pick coins from the box and put them on any one pile
3. Goal: Make all piles have equal number of coins with minimum operations

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only transfer coins between piles and the box, the total number of coins remains constant
2. For all piles to have equal number of coins, each pile must have the same number of coins = total_coins / N
3. The operations needed are the total number of coins that need to be moved from piles to the box or from the box to piles
4. For any target value T, the operations needed = sum of absolute differences between each pile and T

## Strategy

The key insight is that the minimum number of operations occurs when we choose the target value optimally. 

For a set of numbers, the value that minimizes the sum of absolute deviations is the **median** of the array.

However, let me think more carefully:
- If we want all piles to have the same value T, we need to move coins from piles with more than T to the box (outgoing operations)
- We need to add coins to piles with less than T from the box (incoming operations)
- Total operations = sum of all transfers = sum of absolute differences from T to each pile

The minimum sum of absolute differences is achieved when T is the median of the array.

But wait - let me verify with the example:
- Piles: [1, 2, 3, 4]
- Total coins = 10
- Equal coins per pile = 10/4 = 2.5, which isn't an integer

Actually, we don't need the total to be divisible by N. We can have any target value.

Since we want to minimize the total operations, and operations are counted as the total coins transferred, we need to minimize the sum of absolute differences.

The sum of absolute differences is minimized when we choose the median of the array.

For odd N: median is the middle element
For even N: any value between the two middle elements gives the same minimum sum

However, let me check if we can optimize further. Actually, we can prove that the median minimizes the sum of absolute deviations.

## Algorithm

1. Sort the array
2. Find the median (or any median value)
3. Calculate the sum of absolute differences from the median to each element

But actually, we don't need to choose the median specifically - we just need to calculate the minimum possible sum of absolute deviations.

For any target T:
- Operations = sum of absolute values of (A[i] - T) for all i

This is minimized when T is the median of the array.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort the array to easily find median
    arr.sort()
    
    # For minimum sum of absolute deviations, target should be median
    # If n is odd, median is arr[n//2]
    # If n is even, any value between arr[n//2-1] and arr[n//2] gives same sum
    # We can just use arr[n//2] as the median
    
    median_idx = n // 2
    target = arr[median_idx]
    
    # Calculate total operations needed
    total_operations = 0
    for coins in arr:
        total_operations += abs(coins - target)
    
    return total_operations

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

The minimum number of operations to make all piles equal is the sum of absolute differences from the median, which is equivalent to the total number of coins that need to be moved.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    total = sum(arr)
    target = total // n  # Since total coins must be divisible by n
    
    # Calculate sum of absolute differences from target
    operations = 0
    for coins in arr:
        operations += abs(coins - target)
    
    return operations

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

- |1-2| = 1
- |2-2| = 0

- |4-2| = 2
- Total = 1+0+1+2 = 4

Given that the sample output is 3, and my calculation gives 4, I'm missing something.

Wait - what if the target is chosen optimally to minimize the sum of absolute differences?

Still 4.
